Projecting pairwise log-likelihood shifts onto a directed influence graph characterizes each record via three signals: outgoing Exemplifying Advantage, incoming Exemplified Advantage, and a topological One-of-a-Kind Score protecting rare prompts. Pre-filtering restricts LLM twin arbitration to 1%-5% of records while achieving 4.5x enrichment in confirmed errors on HelpSteer2 and 2.2x on Anthropic HH-RLHF, catching contradictions that zero-shot likelihood and margin filters miss. During DPO training, confirmed contradictions oppose the update far more often than clean records (56.7% vs. 37.0% in LoRA gradients) and stay below chance in reward accuracy. Finally, auditing the HH-RLHF benchmark exposes label errors where aligned models disagree with human labels 73%-83% of the time, and retraining DPO after repairing or filtering flagged records improves reward accuracy by +0.5%-0.6% overall and +3.1%-3.5% on repaired inversions, even without LLM arbitration.
  </details>

- **[Learning from All-Failed Groups via First-Visit Coverage in Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22724)**  `arXiv:2607.22724`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kaibing Yang, Guangfeng Cai, Shengtian Yang, Shuo He, Yu Li, Mengyi Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group-based policy optimization has been increasingly used to train large language model (LLM) agents from sparse outcome rewards by comparing trajectories or steps within a group. However, on difficult long-horizon agentic tasks, we found this comparison suffers from a sampling imbalance: repeated or low-effect actions dominate the high-probability region of the policy, leaving useful state-changing actions under-sampled. This imbalance produces many all-failed rollout groups, where the relative advantages within such a group provide no direction for updating the policy. Consequently, the sampling imbalance and the all-failed groups collapse into a self-perpetuating credit trap: failure-dominated sampling yields no learning signal, allowing repeated low-effect actions to persist. To break this trap, we propose Coverage-conditioned Group Policy Optimization (CovGPO), which uses first-visit observation coverage only when all samples in a group receive zero outcome reward. Specifically, within such groups, CovGPO assigns higher relative advantages to state-exploring actions without corrupting the original reward signal, as reaching new observations is a prerequisite for task success. Experiments on two challenging agentic benchmarks, ALFWorld and WebShop, show that CovGPO consistently improves over group-based baselines, with particularly large gains on hard tasks.
  </details>

- **[Improving Certified Robustness via Adversarial Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31653)**  `arXiv:2606.31653`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Matteo Melis, Jesus Martinez Del Rincon, Vishal Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Certified training aims to produce models whose predictions can be formally verified against adversarial perturbations, typically by optimising upper bounds on the worst-case loss over an allowed perturbation set. For neural networks, certified training methods based purely on tight relaxation bounds produce networks that are amenable to certification, but sacrifice standard accuracy. Conversely, adversarial training often yields stronger empirical robustness and standard accuracy, but the resulting models are generally difficult to certify with neural network verifiers. Recently, the literature has shown that better standard-certified accuracy trade-offs can be achieved by combining adversarial training objectives with loose over-approximations based on Interval Bound Propagation (IBP), effectively interpolating between lower and upper bounds of the worst-case loss. Building on this, we introduce AD-CERT, a certified training objective that combines adversarial distillation with an IBP upper bound. We show that distilling adversarial information over the logit space from an empirically robust teacher provides an effective lower bound surrogate for certified training, with AD-CERT achieving state-of-the-art certified performance on several robustness benchmarks for ReLU networks. Furthermore, in a unified setup, distilling adversarial information at the logit-level is shown to improve certified accuracy over a robust feature-space distillation objective by up to 5.40 percentage points. Our code has been made available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Circuit Learning for Interpreting Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16939)**  `arXiv:2606.16939`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Naiyu Yin, Dennis Wei, Tian Gao, Amit Dhurandhar, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy, Yue Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A prominent research direction in mechanistic interpretability is learning sparse circuits over LLM components to reveal how they jointly produce model behavior. However, raw neurons are polysemantic, making learned circuits hard to interpret. Sparse autoencoder (SAE) features alleviate this, but their high dimensionality makes existing intervention-based circuit learning methods computationally prohibitive. We propose CircuitLasso, a scalable circuit learning approach based on sparse linear regression. CircuitLasso recovers circuits whose structural accuracy matches that of state-of-the-art intervention-based methods on the benchmark data, at a fraction of the computational cost. For interpretability, CircuitLasso efficiently uncovers relationships among SAE features, showing how human-interpretable semantic features propagate through the model and influence its predictions. Finally, we validate the utility of our learned circuits by leveraging their insights to achieve comparable performance at substantially lower cost on a domain-generalization task.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic MeanFlow Policies: One-Step Generative Control with Entropic Mirror Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21282)**  `arXiv:2605.21282`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyuan Wang, Da Li, Yulin Chen, Yuehu Gong, Yanming Guo, Liang Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Combining entropy regularisation with mirror descent in online off-policy reinforcement learning requires expressive policies with efficient sampling and explicit entropy control. Gaussian policies struggle to represent multimodal targets, whereas generative policies often require iterative sampling or lack tractable entropy estimates. We introduce Stochastic MeanFlow Policies (SMFP), a one-step generative policy class that combines MeanFlow-based noise-to-action mappings with Gaussian reparameterisation. The latent mapping captures diverse action modes, while a learnable conditional Gaussian scale provides an analytic entropy lower bound. We use this bound to regulate stochasticity alongside direct value guidance and advantage-weighted MeanFlow regression, enabling entropy-regularised mirror-descent learning with one-step policies. Across seven MuJoCo benchmarks, SMFP achieves strong performance against Gaussian and generative baselines with single-step inference. Additional experiments demonstrate multimodal action modelling and effective exploration in sparse-reward navigation.
  </details>

- **[TabMF: Learning Dataset Representations with Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13818)**  `arXiv:2610.13818`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rafiq Kamel, Nicolas Atienza, Aziz Fouché, Nicola Cancedda, Josif Grabocka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Representations of supervised datasets support higher-level machine-learning tasks such as dataset retrieval and distribution-shift monitoring. However, existing approaches typically rely either on hand-crafted meta-features or on dataset encoders trained specifically for representation learning. We introduce TabMF, a framework that repurposes pretrained tabular foundation models (TFMs) as encoders of supervised datasets. TabMF equips a predictive TFM with an architecture-aware dataset-level readout and adapts the model end-to-end using contrastive post-training on stochastic views of synthetic supervised datasets. We instantiate TabMF on TabICLv2 and TabPFN-2.5 and evaluate the resulting representations across six tasks on unseen real datasets. Simple frozen TFM readouts yield competitive representations, indicating that predictive pretraining already induces substantial dataset-level structure. The two TabMF encoders obtain the strongest aggregate performance among the evaluated learned and classical dataset representations, with particularly strong results in dataset retrieval, compact benchmark construction, and distribution-shift severity assessment.
  </details>

- **[SeoulMMOD: A Large-Scale Multimodal Origin-Destination Flow Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13730)**  `arXiv:2610.13730`  `cs.LG`  
  _Taeyoung Yu, Seonbin Jo, Jiwon Kim, Junyoung Byun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Origin-destination (OD) flow forecasting supports urban mobility decisions by predicting the volume and patterns of human movement across a city. Most existing public OD benchmarks provide only a partial view of citywide mobility, covering a single transport service or at most two modes. With partial mode coverage, observed demand changes are difficult to separate into shifts across transport modes and changes in total travel. We introduce SeoulMMOD, a large-scale public benchmark for citywide multimodal mobility in Seoul. SeoulMMOD provides three years of hourly OD flows estimating total mobility for 6 urban travel modes across 25 districts and 426 sub-districts, together with travel-time, travel-distance, calendar, rainfall, and point-of-interest (POI) signals. Using SeoulMMOD, we benchmark 14 forecasting baselines under a common protocol and analyse the effects of spatial scaling, joint versus mode-separate training, and cross-year generalization. Results show that several spatio-temporal models that work at district level do not scale to sub-district OD forecasting, and that naive joint multi-mode training does not consistently improve accuracy. Together, the dataset and benchmark establish a reproducible testbed for multi-mode, multi-year OD forecasting at the city scale.
  </details>

- **[On the Encoding of Calendar Features in Covariate-Informed Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13676)**  `arXiv:2610.13676`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alexander Kreusel, Maximilian Beichter, Alexandra Nikoltchovska, Benjamin Schäfer, Ralf Mikut, Frederik Zahn, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models (TSFMs) increasingly offer covariate support, with calendar information being the most common type of categorical covariate in practice, as it is available for every point in time. However, how these features are encoded is largely treated as an implementation detail rather than as an object of study, despite encoding being one of the few design choices left for pretrained models at inference time. With the present study, we demonstrate that the way information is propagated into the model substantially affects forecast accuracy of TSFMs. Therefore, we systematically compare seven calendar encodings, including three conventional target-agnostic representations (ordinal, one-hot, and cyclic) and four target encodings, across four state-of-the-art TSFMs on nine datasets from multiple domains in a zero-shot rolling evaluation. The nine datasets are influenced by social activities, such as weekends and holidays, making calendar information particularly relevant for forecasting. We find that encoding choice affects forecast accuracy across all models, with MASE differences of up to 9.9 % on non-holidays and 83 % on holidays within a single model. Effectiveness and utilization vary substantially across models. Improvements are concentrated around holidays, where calendar information provides signals unavailable from target history, while some model encoding combinations also improve non-holiday forecasts. A SHAP-based attribution analysis locates the contribution of the covariates relative to the autoregressive component and explains why some model-encoding combinations degrade performance. Our findings suggest that current benchmarks for covariate-informed TSFMs can distort model comparisons by imposing fixed encoding strategies.
  </details>

- **[TabReliBench: Evaluating Tabular Foundation Models Beyond Predictive Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13654)**  `arXiv:2610.13654`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianqi Zhao, Jia Guo, Yan Shuo Tan, Qiong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are increasingly popular for their strong predictive performance. Many also provide posterior predictive distributions (PPDs), whose reliability remains less studied. We assess reliability through point prediction, distributional quality, marginal and subgroup calibration, and interval efficiency. We then introduce TabReliBench, a large classification and regression benchmark for these comparisons and dataset-level analyses. We find that TFMs' rankings change between TabArena and our benchmark and across reliability metrics. Regression TFMs usually achieve near-nominal marginal coverage and retain narrow intervals, but this favorable marginal behavior does not imply valid conditional coverage. Dataset-level analyses show that classification difficulty often accompanies poor calibration, whereas regression predictive performance is not a reliable indicator of calibration. Ensembling improves predictive performance but does not consistently improve calibration of PPDs; TFMs also benefit little from conformal post-processing, which does not reliably resolve subgroup gaps. Reliable TFM selection therefore requires multidimensional evaluation beyond aggregate rankings. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PANDA: Prior-guided Attentional Dual-path Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13635)**  `arXiv:2610.13635`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rishabh Jain, Federico Belotti, Stefano Coniglio, Pietro Lio, Stefano Fiorini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hypergraph Neural Networks (HNNs), designed to model higher-order relations beyond pairwise interactions, typically rely either on fixed structural weights or on normalised attention. Fixed weights can propagate misleading information in heterophilic settings, while normalisation makes attention coefficients competitive, in the sense that, in a message-passing framework, increasing the weight of one sender must reduce the weight of others in the same aggregation set. Our main contribution is the introduction of PANDA (Prior-guided AttentioNal Dual-path Architecture), a plug-in for two-stage message passing HNNs applicable to both undirected and directed settings. The main path interpolates between learned receiver-normalised attention and structural incidence priors, while a non-competitive (not normalised) auxiliary path lets each sender contribute its own transformed information. We also show that, with fixed structural coefficients, PANDA recovers a certain complex-valued convolution operator adopted in previous works. Across four HNNs spanning spatial and spectral methods in both directed and undirected settings, plugging PANDA in yields an average improvement of 5.18 percentage points across eight real-world benchmark datasets. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FedPCD: Priority-Constrained Federated Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13508)**  `arXiv:2610.13508`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yousef Irshaid, Dara Varam, Mohamed I. AlHajri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning (FL) seeks to optimize a shared global model from decentralized client data. While effective for average performance, this process can produce unfair performance across clients, particularly under statistically heterogeneous data distributions. Existing fairness-aware and multi-objective FL methods address this through client re-weighting, gradient correction, or common-descent formulations, but often change the global optimization direction or treat client objectives symmetrically. We propose FedPCD, a priority-constrained federated optimization framework that separates the global optimization objective from client protection. FedPCD preserves the conventional federated aggregate as the primary objective, while allowing only the minimum correction necessary to meet client performance constraints. Across heterogeneous FL benchmarks, FedPCD consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving the highest global accuracy across all configurations and leading worst-10% client accuracy in nearly all. FedPCD further preserves competitive trade-offs between global accuracy and client performance variability. Beyond this trade-off, FedPCD demonstrates faster empirical convergence, reaching matching or superior performance in substantially fewer communication rounds.
  </details>

- **[PPG-Bench: A Unified Benchmark for PPG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13435)**  `arXiv:2610.13435`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arvasu Kulkarni, Mahir Jain, Aditya Ray Mishra, Sandeep Singh, Siddharth Panwar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Photoplethysmography (PPG) is increasingly recorded by wearable and clinical devices at enormous scale, and a growing number of PPG foundation models (FMs) have been developed to compute reusable representations for various downstream tasks. These models are difficult to compare: different evaluations use different datasets, task definitions, preprocessing, downstream heads, hyperparameter tuning, and baselines. We present PPG-Bench, a unifying open-source benchmark evaluating five public PPG FMs across nine open-access datasets and 26 tasks spanning cardiovascular state estimation, activity, sleep, screening, and identification. We evaluate models under four downstream regimes of increasing adaptability (linear probing, random forests, frozen MLP probes, and end-to-end finetuning), with model-specific hyperparameter optimization and subject-disjoint cross-validation. We also evaluate against three controls: random/constant baseline, a deterministic pulse-rate-variability (PRV) feature set, and a generic time-series FM (MOMENT). Across all 68 task-regime combinations, 35.3% show no meaningful FM advantage over the random/constant baseline, and in 83.8% no PPG-specific FM outperforms both PRV and MOMENT. We also find that added downstream head capacity generally improves performance, but can cause significant degradation if the model is performing at chance/constant level. Current PPG FMs therefore cannot yet claim to provide generalised and transferrable representations of PPG signals, and future evaluation in this field should include checks against simple, non-learned controls. Code for the benchmark and instructions to evaluate more models can be found here -this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PlacePulse: Learning Transferable Place Representations from Structural Geographic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13390)**  `arXiv:2610.13390`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lucía García-Duarte, Miguel Álvarez, Diego Vicente, Javier Pérez Trufero, Javier de la Torre_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geospatial machine learning often depends on task-specific feature engineering over large collections of sociodemographic, business, and environmental variables. Geospatial foundation models offer reusable representations of place, but leading approaches typically encode dynamic signals, such as mobility and search activity, or visual signals from satellite imagery. Few models represent the static structural composition of places (who lives and works there, which businesses operate there, and which risks they face) as transferable embeddings at fine spatial resolution. We introduce PlacePulse, a structural geospatial foundation model that compresses 572 attributes, expanded to 2,288 inputs through H3 neighborhood aggregates, into 256-dimensional unit-norm embeddings for 1,055,537 H3 resolution-7 cells in the United States. We also introduce a reusable two-axis benchmark that separates intrinsic representation quality from downstream predictive utility across multiple targets and spatial supports. Intrinsically, the embeddings reconstruct standardized inputs with mean $R^2 = 0.853$, use 251 of 256 approximately decorrelated dimensions, and show strong spatial organization, with Moran's $I = 0.93$ on the leading principal component and autocorrelation persisting beyond the input radius. Extrinsically, across 74 held-out targets from 12 external sources and four spatial supports, PlacePulse outperforms a dimensionality-matched feature-selection baseline on targets unrelated to the training inputs and is competitive with Google's Population Dynamics Foundation Model, leading on health, economic, election, economic-mobility and social-vulnerability outcomes, with relative strengths that vary by domain. These findings position structural composition as a distinct and complementary modality for geospatial foundation models, not simply a fallback where behavioral or imagery data are unavailable.
  </details>

- **[SBNO : Schrödinger Bridge Neural Operator for the Forward and Inverse Problems under Missing Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13317)**  `arXiv:2610.13317`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jaehyeon Park, Inhyeok Jeong, Noseong Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial differential equation (PDE) surrogate modeling often relies on either dense observations or access to governing equations/physics constraints. In practice, observations can be sparse and partial, and the underlying physics may be partially specified or unavailable. We introduce SBNO, an equation-free paired-data diffusion bridge matching framework that learns conditional operators for both forward and inverse PDE problems under partial observations. SBNO uses diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching to learn a pair of time-dependent drifts from a mask-aware endpoint coupling that preserves empirical input-solution pairing during training and supports both PDE-task directions within a single framework. At inference time, SBNO produces a deterministic point reconstruction by integrating the probability-flow ODE constructed from the learned drift pair, without inference-time observation or PDE guidance-weight tuning. On five PDE benchmarks with 5% observations, SBNO achieves superior or comparable accuracy to baselines while demonstrating lower test-instance error dispersion. Furthermore, SBNO is at least $9.7 \times$ faster and reduces GPU memory by at least $4.3 \times$ compared to guided-diffusion baselines.
  </details>

- **[CopRoute: Dependence-Aware Conformal Routing for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13276)**  `arXiv:2610.13276`  `cs.LG`  
  _Siva Rajesh Kasa, Santhosh Kumar Kasa, Karan Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Set-based language model routing aims to increase the coverage of the event where at least one model answers a query correctly while controlling the number of inference calls made. Existing risk-controlled routing policies bound the all-miss risk (the probability that every model in the selected set fails) by calibrating a single conformal threshold and applying it to a nested family of model sets. Because each model is scored in isolation, these policies ignore the dependence structure of model failures. Models that share training data or architecture tend to fail on the same queries, so two individually strong models can be jointly redundant while a weaker but complementary model covers the queries that the others miss. To account for this dependence, which existing risk-controlled policies ignore, we propose CopRoute, which orders models by how many new queries each one answers correctly beyond those already selected, decides per query how far down that order to go, and then picks the smallest cutoff that still meets the target risk. We show that CopRoute's ordering attains, on the design data, the best guarantee any efficient fixed-ordering algorithm can give for the average number of calls to the first correct model, and that a separate calibration step certifies the all-miss risk of the deployed sets. On RouterBench and RouterEval, CopRoute attains the fewest certified calls on nine of ten datasets at a target risk of 0.05 and on eight of ten at 0.10, making up to 79 percent fewer calls (median 24 percent) than the state-of-the-art risk-controlled policy at the same certified all-miss risk.
  </details>

- **[Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Metric Learning for Cross-Head Attention in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13261)**  `arXiv:2610.13261`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenke Duan, Xin Li, Jiqun Pan, Dong Xiaofei, Hanwen Ning, Xinyuan Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Cross-Head Attention (DAMCHA), a simple and effective attention mechanism that generalizes Multi-Head Attention (MHA) through the lens of metric learning. Standard MHA implicitly learns a static, head-wise Euclidean similarity corresponding to the diagonal blocks of a unified cross-head metric matrix, discarding all off-diagonal interactions across heads. DAMCHA addresses this limitation via two key innovations. First, it learns the full cross-head metric matrix, inducing a Mahalanobis-like similarity that explicitly captures inter-head interactions beyond the restricted block-diagonal structure of MHA. Second, it parameterizes the metric matrix as an input-dependent function, enabling the attention geometry to dynamically adapt to the intrinsic manifold structure of the data rather than remaining fixed after training. We further introduce principled regularization strategies, most notably stack-wise parameter sharing, to ensure computational efficiency and stable optimization. DAMCHA serves as a drop-in replacement for MHA and its variants in various Transformer architectures, delivering superior expressive power and learning performance with comparable or reduced computational overhead. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DAMCHA-based Transformers consistently outperform competing baselines across a range of benchmark tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scaling RNA Foundation Models through Iterative Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13106)**  `arXiv:2610.13106`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pengan Chen, Liang Hong, Xinyuan Liu, Han Huang, Ziqian Lin, Jiayang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biological function in RNA is encoded across scales, from local sequence motifs to long-range structural dependencies. Conventional RNA foundation models couple parameter capacity and inference depth within a fixed architecture, prescribing a fixed computation graph for each inference. Here we present Eco-RNA, an 80M-parameter RNA foundation model that makes inference depth adjustable through learned iterative refinement. Its shared, input-conditioned Transformer core refines sequence representations through repeated application. The iteration count is selected after training and controls effective depth at fixed model parameters. We train across recurrent depths with masked reconstruction, state regularization, and a one-sided loss that anchors deeper reconstruction to a shallow reference. On the 70-assay RNAGym fitness benchmark, increasing refinement from one to four iterations raises aggregate absolute Spearman correlation from 0.242 to 0.286, with the gain retained through eight iterations. At four iterations, Eco-RNA surpasses the current SOTA Evo 2 40B on all three aggregate fitness metrics despite 500x fewer parameters. Its iteration configurations advance the compute-performance Pareto frontier among the evaluated models. It also outperforms the evaluated baselines in non-coding RNA family classification, RNA secondary-structure prediction, and chemical probing profile prediction. These results establish internal inference computation as an effective scaling dimension for RNA foundation models.
  </details>

- **[CB-Orchestrator: Adaptive Workflow Optimization for LLM Agents via Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13064)**  `arXiv:2610.13064`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiahang Sun, Zhiwei Shang, Zhipiao Liu, Hongwei Yang, Guoqing Xie, Shuang Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in tackling complex tasks through agentic workflows. However, manually designing these workflows is labor-intensive and lacks flexibility to adapt to diverse queries. While automated workflow optimization is a promising direction, existing methods often incur expensive API costs during training-phase feedback collection. Furthermore, they fail to adaptively reuse high-quality workflows, which creates a bottleneck for both performance and cost-effectiveness. To address these limitations, we propose CB-Orchestrator, an adaptive framework that decouples workflow generation from selection. In the first stage, we construct a diverse pool of candidate workflows via evolutionary search. In the second stage, we formulate workflow selection as a Contextual Bandit problem, which enables sample-efficient learning by balancing exploration and exploitation, thereby significantly reducing the feedback required for training. During inference, the model adaptively selects the optimal workflow tailored to each query. Evaluations across five diverse benchmarks demonstrate that CB-Orchestrator consistently outperforms all baselines. Notably, on the MATH dataset, CB-Orchestrator achieves better performance than strong recent baselines while reducing training-phase API token overhead by no less than 40.55% and total end-to-end training (including workflow pool generation) token overhead by no less than 29.73%.
  </details>

- **[Towards Easier-to-Tune Training Algorithms: New Results from AlgoPerf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13036)**  `arXiv:2610.13036`  `cs.LG`  
  _Priya Kasimbeg, Ahmed Khaled, Aahlad Chandrabhatta, Yifan Wang, Andy Liu, Niccolò Ajroldi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating deep learning optimizer is challenging because confounding factors such as hyperparameter tuning and optimizer-model co-design often obscure true algorithmic performance. We present results of the updated AlgoPerf V1 benchmark, in which we evaluate a new set of training algorithms on wall-clock time-to-target across nine workloads on fixed hardware. This report focuses on the results of the benchmark's self-tuning ruleset, which requires submissions to use a single configuration across all workloads. Out of the 13 submissions in PyTorch and JAX, Schedule-Free AdamW ranked first. In addition to the wall-clock scores, we also analyze the trade-offs between step efficiency and wall-clock execution, revealing that matrix-preconditioned and two-loop methods achieve good sample efficiency but require more aggressive computational optimizations to be competitive in runtime. Finally, our hyperparameter saliency and retention analyses show that the learning rate and momentum are highly sensitive across domains and algorithms, highlighting the need for methods that are more robust against hyperparameter tuning and can generalize well to new tasks.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13020)**  `arXiv:2610.13020`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhicheng Cai, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, Hao Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability.
  </details>

- **[Evidential Multimodal Transformers with Dempster-Shafer Fusion for Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13013)**  `arXiv:2610.13013`  `cs.LG`  
  _Muhammad Usama, Yeoreum Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Softmax-based multimodal emotion recognition methods often assign high confidence even when sensor data are corrupted or contradictory, for example when facial expressions suggest positive valence while vocal prosody indicates negative arousal. We present an Evidential Multimodal Transformer with Dempster-Shafer Fusion (EMT-DS) that addresses this problem through formal uncertainty quantification. EMT-DS models predictions as Subjective Opinions (Dirichlet distributions), separating epistemic uncertainty (lack of evidence) from aleatoric uncertainty (sensor noise). Modality-specific transformer encoders produce belief mass distributions that are fused with Dempster's Rule of Combination, which normalizes the joint belief mass by a conflict coefficient 1/(1-c), so that contradictory evidence is explicitly quantified as conflict and the fused prediction becomes diffuse rather than being forced into an unjustified consensus. Under subject-independent and speaker-independent evaluation on four benchmarks, EMT-DS achieves 84.3% accuracy on IEMOCAP versus 81.7% for the best baseline (p < 0.01), 82.4% on DEAP under leave-one-subject-out evaluation versus 78.1%, and 96.8% on the highly imbalanced KEMDy20, and it improves accuracy on CMU-MOSEI by 2.4 points over the strongest evidential baseline. In controlled ablation, it reduces Expected Calibration Error from 0.153 (softmax transformer) to 0.042. It maintains 71.2% accuracy under severe Gaussian noise on audio features (sigma = 1.0), where attention baselines drop to 45.3%, and degrades gracefully when entire modalities are absent. The fusion step has O(MK^2) complexity, compared with O(M^2 d^2) for cross-modal attention. The learned conflict metric also flags likely errors: reviewing 20% of samples captures 80% of misclassifications, which supports selective prediction in clinical and safety-critical systems.
  </details>

- **[Controlled-Minimax: Stabilizing Adversarial Optimization via Control Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12915)**  `arXiv:2610.12915`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianjiao Luo, Jianfei Chen, Jun Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Minimax optimization is fundamental to adversarial learning yet suffers from severe stability challenges, often manifesting as persistent limit cycles or divergence. This paper investigates these instabilities through the lens of continuous-time dynamical systems. By modeling training dynamics as a second-order Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE), we rigorously identify the root cause of non-convergence: the problematic eigenstructure of the loss function's Hessian near equilibrium points. To address this, we introduce Controlled-Minimax, a novel stabilization framework that reformulates the optimization trajectory as a controllable physical system. Unlike heuristic regularization techniques, our approach derives a specific feedback controller that provides theoretical guarantees of local exponential stability, regardless of the underlying Hessian spectrum. Consequently, our method stabilizes general minimax problems where traditional gradient descent-ascent methods fail. Extensive experiments on pathological benchmarks including the Dirac-GAN, 2D Gaussian mixtures, and Swiss Roll datasets, as well as high-resolution image synthesis with StyleGAN2-ada, demonstrate that Controlled-Minimax effectively suppresses oscillatory behavior while ensuring fast and robust convergence.
  </details>

- **[GIST: Generator-state-Informed Supervision for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12904)**  `arXiv:2610.12904`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhangyang Peng, Jingze Shi, Tongyi Zhang, Yuyu Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) based on prior-data fitted networks typically use sampled hard query labels, leaving avoidable sampling variability in pretraining. A key observation is that intermediate generator states can reveal label uncertainty beyond a sampled outcome. We propose Generator-state-Informed Supervision for TFMs (GIST), which uses these states to average query losses over conditional label distributions. GIST jointly designs task generation and query supervision, using entropy-calibrated encodings to obtain tractable, input-consistent targets on supported label-dependent branches. We show that this averaging preserves the population objective and yields no larger gradient covariance at fixed parameters within each generating distribution. Under matched pretraining schedules, GIST improves mean rank across three architectures on both TabArena and TALENT without changing architecture or inference. Across these benchmarks and architectures, GIST wins 62.1% of paired dataset-level comparisons against the corresponding hard-label controls.
  </details>

- **[CoGameReg: Cooperative-Game-Based Collaborative Semi-Supervised Regression for Seismic Reservoir Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12884)**  `arXiv:2610.12884`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyu Guo, Jiwei Jia, Lei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate estimation of reservoir properties from seismic data remains challenging when well-log labels are sparse but three-dimensional seismic observations are abundant. We present CoGameReg, a cooperative-game-based semi-supervised regression framework that uses contribution-weighted predictions of heterogeneous regressors to construct shared pseudo-labels. This design makes each regressor's influence explicit in the learning targets generated for unlabeled seismic samples under sparse well control. The Tri-Game Shapley mechanism combines a rank-MSE performance game with a pairwise consistency game to estimate nonnegative regressor weights from labeled training data. These weights remain fixed during collaborative training and are reused for final prediction fusion. Candidate pseudo-labels are screened using a common local k-nearest-neighbor error-reduction criterion before retraining. We evaluate CoGameReg on 10 public regression datasets, a synthetic three-dimensional P-wave velocity prediction task, and a field water-saturation application. The benchmark advantage is most pronounced at the 2.5% labeled-data ratio and decreases as supervision increases. CoGameReg obtains the best blind-well metrics in the synthetic experiment. In the field application, predictions clipped to 0-100% yield a pooled coefficient of determination of 0.5942, a root mean square error of 5.7441 percentage points, and a Pearson correlation of 0.8265 across three blind wells. CoGameReg has the highest Pearson correlation among the four field methods. The results support the use of CoGameReg to integrate sparse well labels with dense seismic attributes, with performance varying across metrics and well locations.
  </details>

- **[HART: Hybrid Attention-RWKV Time series Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12883)**  `arXiv:2610.12883`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qingwen Lin, Boyan Xu, Shifeng Xie, Zhifeng Hao, Keli Zhang, Ruichu Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Non-autoregressive multi-step forecasting (NAF) enables time series foundation models to predict an entire future horizon in a single forward pass. Despite its strong sequence modeling capabilities, RWKV-7 exhibits long-horizon degradation in our NAF experiments. In this setup, future patches are represented as missing inputs, requiring the recurrent state to evolve across the prediction horizon without new observations. Our analysis of RWKV-7's generalized delta rule suggests a mechanism we term Placeholder Idling Decay (PID): repeated state transitions can attenuate historical information when placeholder-driven writes provide insufficient compensation. Layer-wise analysis reveals reduced state-injection norms in most layers, while transition-matrix norms remain similar between historical and prediction regions, consistent with PID. Motivated by this analysis, we propose HART, a Hybrid Attention-RWKV Time-series Foundation Model that alternates attention and RWKV blocks, combining direct access to historical tokens with recurrent temporal modeling. Controlled experiments on GIFT-Eval and TIME-Benchmark show that HART improves over pure RWKV at medium and long horizons while maintaining comparable short-horizon performance, and achieves better aggregate performance than a comparably sized pure Transformer. With 36M parameters, HART achieves long-horizon MASE comparable to Toto-2.0-1B on GIFT-Eval (0.752 vs. 0.754), using approximately 1/28 of its parameters.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization: Unified Learning from Offline Demonstrations and On- and Off-Policy Rollouts via a Shared Regression Target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12830)**  `arXiv:2610.12830`  `cs.LG`  
  _Du Cheng, Junyu Lu, Jingfan Zhang, Yuzhuo Zhang, Haoxuan Jia, Shichao Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning post-training draws on offline demonstrations and policy-generated rollouts, yet conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) separates their objectives and limits continued reuse of collected responses. We propose \textbf{Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization} (CBPO), which makes these sources compatible with one regression loss through a fixed-reference target. Because the generating policy does not enter this target, positive and negative offline completions, reference responses, and current and historical rollouts can share a common reward-labeled record format and replay buffer. As new responses enter the buffer, optimization retains the same regression objective without coordinating separate SFT and RL objectives or requiring stored behavior-policy probabilities, importance weighting, or a learned critic. Restricting the available sources gives offline, on-policy, and off-policy variants, while CBPO-Unified combines them. Across two model scales and four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CBPO delivers leading \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} performance across benchmarks and data regimes. On Qwen3-1.7B, CBPO-Unified improves benchmark-averaged \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} over SFT DAPO by 2.33 and 4.44 percentage points, respectively. In the replay ablation, retaining historical rollouts improves these metrics by 1.87 and 3.72 percentage points over the no-history configuration.
  </details>

- **[TreeHCA: Hindsight-Guided Exploration and Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12828)**  `arXiv:2610.12828`  `cs.LG`  
  _Debajoy Mukherjee, Jeremy Carleton, Sushil Vemuri, Srinivas Shakkottai, Dileep Kalathil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Outcome-supervised group-relative policy optimization (GRPO) assigns the same advantage to every token in an LLM agent trajectory, leaving useful and unhelpful decisions undifferentiated. Hindsight credit assignment (HCA) can refine this feedback, but its estimates rely on trajectories containing informative alternatives at consequential histories. We introduce TreeHCA, which couples hindsight-guided exploration with hindsight credit assignment. TreeHCA uses changes in reference-answer likelihood to revisit intermediate histories and sample alternative continuations, forming a tree of reasoning and tool interactions. Because this exploration changes the distribution of sampled continuations, we derive the corresponding inverse-hindsight importance correction, including an exact finite-pool identity. We use this structure in a normalized tree backup that propagates terminal outcomes into turn-level advantages. This shared hindsight structure is TreeHCA's central design principle, linking informative exploration with hindsight-guided credit assignment. Across seven question-answering benchmarks and three Qwen backbones, TreeHCA outperforms all baselines in aggregate exact match, validating the effectiveness of paired exploration and credit assignment for multi-turn LLM agent training. Our code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Provenance-grouped protocol for evaluating machine learning in perovskite photovoltaics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12766)**  `arXiv:2610.12766`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qiwei Han, Chi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine-learning models for perovskite solar cells are typically evaluated using random data splits, which we show systematically leak information through massive internal duplication. Because nearly two-thirds of curated devices share their exact recipe with another, random splits primarily reward the mere recall of historical data rather than testing true physical generalisation. To solve this, we mapped the institutional provenance of the Perovskite Database and established a rigorous hierarchy of grouped splits that progressively isolate duplicate devices, publications, and institutions. Benchmarking thirteen models establishes new state-of-the-art performance across all splits and reveals a critical regime shift: while tree ensembles dominate random splits by recognising familiar recipes, their ranking collapses on genuinely unseen institutions and future discoveries, where composition-aware neural architectures excel. Furthermore, feature importance demonstrates that the device architecture and fabrication methodology dictate final efficiency just as strongly as the absorber chemistry. This standardised protocol empowers the community to separate memorisation from true physical discovery.
  </details>

- **[NV-Tesseract-AD: Curriculum-Enhanced Conditional Diffusion for Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12754)**  `arXiv:2610.12754`  `cs.LG`  
  _Aditi Gautam, Manikandan Ravikiran, Saira Qureshi, Sena Fatma Ekiz, Charan Jagwani, Mahmoud Elhadidy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose NV-Tesseract-AD, a curriculum learning-enhanced conditional diffusion framework for multivariate time-series anomaly detection. Our central hypothesis is that reconstruction difficulty can be explicitly structured along two complementary axes, masking severity and diffusion noise level, and that training from easier to harder tasks along both axes simultaneously stabilizes optimization and improves residual discriminability. We integrate a Transformer-based diffusion backbone with a three-stage dual curriculum over masking ratios and diffusion timesteps, retaining anomalous samples throughout training and relying on structured corruption to drive self-supervised representation learning. We evaluate NV-Tesseract-AD under two regimes: in-distribution training on the TSB-UAD-M corpus and strict zero-shot transfer to ten EasyTSAD benchmark families without per-dataset tuning. The dual-axis curriculum produces masking-invariant reconstruction, confirmed by a near-zero loss gap between complementary masking strategies within the first training stage, and yields an approximately 3.7-fold improvement in F1 and a 56 percent reduction in MSE relative to a non-curriculum baseline. Zero-shot performance reaches F1 = 0.984 on NAB and F1 = 0.948 on NormA. Weaker results on heterogeneous families are traced to unsupervised threshold calibration rather than reconstruction quality, identifying calibration as the dominant remaining detection bottleneck. Accelerated inference via DPM-Solver++ reduces sampling cost by a factor of 3.5 to 5.7 at the dataset level but couples to thresholding behavior, establishing that the sampler and threshold must be validated jointly. Together, these findings demonstrate that aligning diffusion denoising difficulty with curriculum learning principles provides a principled and practical approach for robust anomaly detection in complex multivariate time series.
  </details>

- **[A Categorical Atlas of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning: Cones, Orbits, and Gauges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12728)**  `arXiv:2610.12728`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Li, Zhizhou Sha, Chiwun Yang, Dai Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning has hundreds of methods, usually compared by parameter count and benchmark score. We compare them as maps. A pretrained checkpoint is a base point in weight space, and a weight-space method is a smooth map from a small parameter space that starts at that point. These maps form a slice category in which the frozen model is initial and full fine-tuning terminal. Three invariants of the map do most of the work. Its image near the base point governs expressivity, through the first-order image, the dimension, a min-cut bound on rank and whether that point is smooth or an apex. Its fibres, with the optimizer's metric, govern training dynamics. Base change moves the base point and covers splitting initialisations, quantisation, restarts and merging. We prove, for example, that zero-initialised LoRA, and Householder reflection adaptation at its default start under stated rank and parity conditions, begin at the apex of a cone; that no exact initialisation lets LoRA reach an update of rank above twice its own; that exactly orthogonal methods never change a weight's singular values in exact arithmetic, however often they are merged; that under Adam with no epsilon, weight decay or clipping and one shared schedule, LoRA+ from the standard zero initialisation follows exactly the trajectory of plain LoRA with its scale multiplied by the learning-rate ratio; that Adam with shared hyperparameters respects only the signed permutations inside LoRA's continuous symmetry group of full-rank factors; and that the activation function decides when a scaling adapter folds into preceding weights. We classify 382 methods from 138 papers by seven coordinates read off their formulas, with 403 simulation arrows and 485 obstructions. Every numbered result is proved in full, and results due to others are credited where we re-derive them.
  </details>

- **[Data-driven Optimal Sensor Placement for Aerosol Monitoring in the UK](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12658)**  `arXiv:2610.12658`  `cs.LG`  
  _Talha Ansar, Waqar Muhammad Ashraf, Alejandro Coca-Castro, Manvendra Janmaijaya, Ramit Debnath, J. Scott Hosking_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We develop a data-driven sensor-placement framework and apply it to aerosol monitoring in the United Kingdom (UK). A trained convolutional Gaussian neural process is embedded within the DeepSensor optimisation framework. Genetic optimisation with lazy evaluation and caching reduces mean computational time by 60\% (10$\pm$3 versus 25$\pm$7 minutes) while producing sequential placement performance comparable to the greedy solver. Using the CAMS reference field to construct a retrospective oracle benchmark, Joint NLL improves by about 22\% for Joint-MI-driven genetic placement after five locations for aerosol stations are identified. Seasonal and wind-regime analyses show that agreement between model-based criteria and oracle benchmarks varies with meteorological transport conditions. These results show a computationally efficient approach for data-informed expansion of environmental observing systems.
  </details>

- **[SynAct: Synergistic Active Feedback Learning for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12651)**  `arXiv:2610.12651`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ning Liu, Ian Li, Kristina Klinkner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents executing multi-step workflows fail in two fundamentally different ways: recoverable slips, which further attempts can repair, and knowledge gaps, which retrying cannot resolve. Expert feedback can address the latter, but expert attention is scarce. The key challenge is therefore not whether to ask for feedback, but where feedback can change the outcome. Existing approaches either avoid expert feedback, allowing agents to reinforce their own errors, or allocate feedback at the task level without identifying consequential decisions. We present SynAct, an active learning framework that treats expert attention as the binding constraint and allocates feedback at the level of individual decisions. SynAct ranks decisions by the mutual information between the selected action and the terminal outcome across repeated rollouts, queries an expert at the highest-value decisions, and distills the resulting corrections into transferable rules that the agent can reuse without expert access at deployment. We theoretically characterize what this signal identifies under finite repeated sampling: with k rollouts and at least m observations per action, outcome-constant decisions have zero mutual information, while outcome-separating decisions have mutual information at least H2(m/k) bits, yielding exact separation for any threshold t in (0,H2(m/k)). This also explains why success-based acquisition can be blind when all rollouts fail: success is constant even when failed runs reach different outcomes. Across four agent benchmarks and four models spanning three families, SynAct improves held-out accuracy in all 16 evaluated settings, by +11.7pp on average. The same signal transfers to inference-time routing, where SynAct outperforms budget-matched cascade, uncertainty, and expert-judge routers in 15 of 16 settings, while a single targeted correction outperforms seven additional unaimed retries.
  </details>

- **[Realism VS Accuracy: Event Sequence Forecasting from a Generative Modeling Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12586)**  `arXiv:2610.12586`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dmitry Osin, Egor Surkov, Petr Mokrov, Igor Udovichenko, Ivan Karpukhin, Evgeny Burnaev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event sequences are chains of random events, each described by heterogeneous (numerical and categorical) features. The problem of event sequence forecasting occurs in finance, healthcare, business process intelligence, and other fields. In this work, we study multi-horizon forecasting of event sequences as a conditional generative modeling problem. We evaluate forecasts not only for prediction accuracy, but also for distributional realism. To this end, we present a benchmark comprising metrics for both quality aspects, strong statistical baselines, recent models adapted from adjacent fields, and curated real-world datasets. Careful evaluation reveals that no method excels at both accuracy and realism, highlighting a fundamental trade-off between the two. Finally, we introduce Wasserstein BaryBooster, an algorithm that aggregates samples from a realism-strong diffusion model into a state-of-the-art predictor, demonstrating that distributional realism can be converted into prediction accuracy. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Behind-the-Meter Photovoltaic Visibility from Net Smart Meters: Deployment Regimes and Operational Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12527)**  `arXiv:2610.12527`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jack Zheng, Hao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distribution operators need visibility of behind-the-meter photovoltaic (PV) generation, yet most residential customers are observed only through net smart meters. Deep-learning-based PV disaggregation can restore this visibility, but its operational value depends on whether the model serves known customers, new customers, future periods, or new regions. We quantify this dependence using four deployment regimes that separate household transfer from temporal extrapolation, applied to Ausgrid smart-meter data with cross-dataset adaptation to the Pecan Street data. Using a fixed training protocol, we benchmark representative deep sequence architectures for PV disaggregation under regime-matched evaluation. At a fixed 48-hour context length, daylight mean absolute error (MAE) of the best model rises from 0.104~kW on seen households in the future test period to 0.185~kW on unseen households over the same period, and under a fixed training contract the deployment regime explains far more of the error variation than model choice. Under target-domain normalization, fine-tuning on five target-region households reduces source-model transfer error by 28\%--36\%. Long input windows help known-customer extrapolation, while their new-customer benefits vary by model. These findings suggest regime-matched error budgets, limited target supervision for model adaptation in new regions, and use-case-dependent context selection for energy data analytics.
  </details>

- **[Algebraic Integer Quantization for Machine Learning Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12494)**  `arXiv:2610.12494`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anthony Kostal-Vazquez, Vassil Dimitrov, Branden Butler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this article, we propose algebraic integer number formats (AINF) as a framework for highly configurable and accurate compressed computation. A key property of AINF is its straightforward reduction to purely integer arithmetic, enabling error-free multiply-accumulate operations. We develop a matrix multiplication engine that exploits this property, obtaining performance within a predictable factor of the most efficient numerical counterparts on modern hardware accelerators. Another key advantage of AIQ is the freedom to select algebraic integers tailored to the numerical characteristics of a given use case, offering optimization opportunities that are fundamentally unavailable in floating-point or fixed-point formats. Motivated by these properties, we introduce algebraic integer quantization (AIQ), a lossy compression format for large language model (LLM) inference, and provide results demonstrating that AIQ achieves competitive accuracy compared to the industry-standard MX format across a diverse set of models and benchmarks at equivalent bit-per-parameter budgets. The experiments presented in this article employ algebraic integers of degree two (quadratic integers); generalizations to higher-order and, in particular, multidimensional algebraic integers promise even greater computational advantages and remain a promising direction for future work.
  </details>

- **[When to Intervene? State-Aware Sparse Manipulation in Federated Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11523)**  `arXiv:2610.11523`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shutong Zheng, Sijia Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated reinforcement learning (FRL) enables distributed agents to collaboratively train decision-making policies, but its decentralized training process also exposes global policy learning to Byzantine manipulation. Existing poisoning attacks primarily focus on how to construct malicious updates, while trajectory-level intervention timing remains largely implicit. In sequential decision making, however, where an intervention is applied can alter subsequent trajectories and learning signals. Through controlled experiments, we find that changing the selected trajectory states materially alters attack efficacy even when the malicious-update construction is fixed. We therefore identify when as a distinct attack dimension and introduce the Viability-constrained Behavioral Steering Attack (V-BSA), which uses local policy uncertainty to select sparse intervention states and applies envelope-constrained behavioral steering. Across discrete-action benchmarks, V-BSA achieves substantial degradation against robust aggregators and ensemble defenses with only a fraction of the interventions used by dense poisoning, while revealing task- and aggregation-dependent boundaries. Overall, our results highlight intervention timing as a distinct dimension of sequential robustness in FRL. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[LLM4Trust: Exploring the Capabilities of Large Language Models for Trust Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33521)**  `arXiv:2609.33521`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jie Wang, Yanbo Sun, Zheng Yan, Jiahe Lan, Elisa Bertino_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trust evaluation plays a critical role in cybersecurity by supporting risk mitigation and decision-making. A variety of trust evaluation methods have been proposed, with learning-based approaches offering high accuracy and automation. However, they often require substantial ground truth, suffer from low training efficiency, lack support for basic trust properties, and provide limited explainability. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a compelling alternative due to their strong zero-/few-shot reasoning abilities and broad knowledge. To this end, we propose LLM4Trust, the first benchmark framework that systematically explores the capabilities of LLMs for trust evaluation. We first construct diverse trust graphs to model five basic trust properties and design corresponding property understanding tasks. We then assess the ability of eight representative LLMs to understand these properties under nine prompt methods. Based on this exploration, we identify the most effective LLM-prompt combinations and apply them to five real-world datasets for validating LLMs' trust evaluation capability. During this process, we propose two strategies to extract key information from large-scale trust graphs, addressing the context window limitations of LLMs. Extensive experiments show that LLMs can effectively understand basic trust properties and have great potential for real-world trust evaluation, particularly under limited supervision. However, they remain vulnerable to attacks targeting trust graphs and demonstration examples used in few-shot prompting, and incur high inference costs. Accordingly, we propose a defense mechanism and batch inference to improve the robustness and efficiency of LLM-based trust evaluation. The source code of LLM4Trust is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Attention for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31306)**  `arXiv:2609.31306`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maximilian Schambach, Clemens Biehl, Sam Thelin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular in-context learners such as TabPFN, Mitra, or ConTextTab rely on alternating row and column attention over 2D sequences of latent embeddings. These attention patterns differ markedly from the one-dimensional case in language models: row attention involves longer sequences while column attention operates on much shorter ones, and the strided memory layout of tabular data makes producing contiguous tensors costly. Moreover, the hidden dimensions used in current models are small compared to recent language models. Yet efficient attention has been studied mostly for one-dimensional sequences, leaving the two-dimensional tabular setting unexplored. To this end, we create a reproducible benchmarking setup and study the unique characteristics of tabular attention across several backends -- Torch SDPA (efficient and cuDNN), FlashAttention-2/3/4, and the inference-only backends vLLM and SageAttention -- measuring forward and backward throughput across realistic tabular shapes on three GPU generations (A100, H100, B200). We find that the optimal backend choice differs between column and row attention and varies across hardware as well as model specifics: While the FlashAttention implementations tailored for each GPU generation perform overall best, they are at times outperformed by CuDNN in the case of column attention at longer sequences with cross-over points depending on the head dimension. Among inference-only backends, SageAttention performs well for row attention and large sequences beyond 16\,k rows. Our reproducible benchmark lays the foundation for future improvements to table-native attention. The self-contained benchmarking and evaluation code is openly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[A Unified Benchmark for Dynamic Medical Treatment Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01028)**  `arXiv:2606.01028`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuepeng Wang, Ken Kawano, Yoshihiko Fujisawa, Yongqi Zhou, Akifumi Wachi, Mehrshad Sadria, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical treatment recommendation poses several challenges to reinforcement learning (RL): patient physiology evolves in continuous time, measurements and interventions are performed at irregular intervals, and treatment effects vary substantially across individuals. Existing RL formulations and simulated environments, however, are based on discrete-time MDPs with fixed decision intervals. Thus, it remains difficult to evaluate whether RL methods can handle time-interval-dependent disease progression, personalized treatment response, and safety between consecutive measurement points. To address this gap, we introduce MedGym, a benchmark environment for dynamic treatment recommendation. MedGym models longitudinal patient evolution in a continuous-time framework and constructs a configurable medical RL benchmark from clinical data by using Physics-Informed Neural Networks. The resulting benchmark enables direct comparison between discrete-time and continuous-time methods under irregular treatment timing and patient-specific dynamics. Furthermore, MedGym supports evaluation from clinically important perspectives, such as personalization and trajectory-level safety. By providing a standardized and configurable benchmark for continuous-time dynamic treatment, MedGym enables more realistic and informative evaluation of medical RL methods.
  </details>

- **[Do Deep Ensembles Actually Capture Uncertainty in Graph Neural Networks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22593)**  `arXiv:2605.22593`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pedro C. Vieira, Viacheslav Borovitskiy, Pedro Ribeiro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While deep ensembles are widely considered to be the default method for uncertainty quantification in deep learning, their effectiveness for graph-structured data is often simply assumed based on successes in domains like computer vision. We investigate standard deep ensembles specifically for message-passing graph neural networks. Benchmarking across eight datasets representing varied tasks and complexities, we reveal that ensembles provide only marginal improvement over a single model. These marginal gains stem primarily from averaging out optimization noise in point predictions rather than from meaningfully better uncertainty estimates. Through an aleatoric-epistemic decomposition, we identify epistemic collapse: independently trained networks consistently converge to overly similar predictions. Because disagreement is the fundamental mechanism through which ensembles capture epistemic uncertainty, this lack of diversity neutralizes their key advantage. Towards explaining this phenomenon, we derive a bound on epistemic variance in terms of the graph topology and Dirichlet energy budget, linking epistemic collapse to the smoothing bias of message passing. Our results suggest that deep ensemble success does not seamlessly transfer to graph machine learning.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Nonparametric Mixed-Effect ODEs with Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13088)**  `arXiv:2605.13088`  `cs.LG`  
  _Julien Martinelli, Maksim Sinelnikov, Harri Lähdesmäki, Quentin Clairon, Mélanie Prague_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamical modelling in pharmacometrics, systems biology, and epidemiology often involves subjects with related but distinct dynamics, observed through temporally sparse and irregular measurements. Classical nonlinear mixed-effects ODE models represent this heterogeneity but rely on parametric vector fields, making them vulnerable to structural misspecification. Existing nonparametric ODE methods typically assume shared dynamics rather than an explicit mixed-effect hierarchy. We propose MEGPODE, a Bayesian nonparametric mixed-effect ODE model that decomposes each subject's vector field into a shared population component and a subject-specific deviation, both endowed with Gaussian process priors. To avoid repeated ODE solves during training, we represent latent trajectories with state-space GP priors and penalize discrepancies between their derivatives and the vector fields on a finite time grid. A single variational objective enables approximate joint inference over trajectories and vector fields, accounting for state uncertainty and dependence between population and subject-specific dynamics. Across heterogeneous oscillatory and biomedical ODE benchmarks, MEGPODE improves population-field recovery and subject-level trajectory prediction relative to strong baselines.
  </details>

- **[Bridge Matching Sampler: Scalable Sampling via Generalized Fixed-Point Diffusion Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00530)**  `arXiv:2603.00530`  `cs.LG`  
  _Denis Blessing, Lorenz Richter, Julius Berner, Egor Malitskiy, Gerhard Neumann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sampling from unnormalized densities using diffusion models has emerged as a powerful paradigm. However, while recent approaches that use least-squares `matching' objectives have improved scalability, they often necessitate significant trade-offs, such as restricting prior distributions or relying on unstable optimization schemes. By generalizing these methods as special forms of fixed-point iterations rooted in Nelson's relation, we develop a new method that addresses these limitations, called Bridge Matching Sampler (BMS). Our approach enables learning a stochastic transport map between arbitrary prior and target distributions with a single, scalable, and stable objective. Furthermore, we introduce a damped variant of this iteration that incorporates a regularization term to mitigate mode collapse and further stabilize training. Empirically, we demonstrate that our method enables sampling at unprecedented scales while preserving mode diversity, achieving state-of-the-art results on complex synthetic densities and high-dimensional molecular benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Deep End-to-End Survival Analysis with Temporal Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06786)**  `arXiv:2410.06786`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mariana Vargas Vieyra, Pascal Frossard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this study, we present a novel Survival Analysis algorithm designed to efficiently handle large-scale longitudinal data. Our approach draws inspiration from Reinforcement Learning principles, particularly the Deep Q-Network paradigm, extending Temporal Learning concepts to Survival Regression. A central argument in our method relies on temporal consistency, a recursive consistency condition between survival predictions made at consecutive states. Our framework introduces a delayed target network that decouples the generation of temporally consistent training targets from model updates, providing a more stable training signal while enabling mini-batch optimization and end-to-end training. Additionally, it supports arbitrarily complex architectures, enabling the modeling of intricate temporal dependencies. Through numerous experiments we provide empirical evidence demonstrating our framework's ability to exploit temporal consistency across datasets of varying sizes. Moreover, our algorithm outperforms benchmarks on datasets with long sequences, demonstrating its ability to capture long-term patterns. Finally, ablation studies show that the degree of target delay has a substantial effect on predictive performance and training variability. Across two distinct survival model families, delayed target updates consistently improve over the same-timescale baseline.
  </details>

- **[TernaryLLM: Ternarized Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07177)**  `arXiv:2406.07177`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianqi Chen, Zhe Li, Weixiang Xu, Zeyu Zhu, Dong Li, Lu Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks, but they are hindered by high computational costs and memory requirements. Ternarization, an extreme form of quantization, offers a solution by reducing memory usage and enabling energy-efficient floating-point additions. However, applying ternarization to LLMs faces challenges stemming from outliers in both weights and activations. In this work, observing asymmetric outliers and non-zero means in weights, we introduce Dual Learnable Ternarization (DLT), which enables both scales and shifts to be learnable. We also propose Outlier-Friendly Feature Knowledge Distillation (OFF) to recover the information lost in extremely low-bit quantization. The proposed OFF can incorporate semantic information and is insensitive to outliers. At the core of OFF is maximizing the mutual information between features in ternarized and floating-point models using cosine similarity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our TernaryLLM surpasses previous low-bit quantization methods on the standard text generation and zero-shot benchmarks for different LLM families. Specifically, for one of the most powerful open-source models, LLaMA-3, our approach (W1.58A16) outperforms the previous state-of-the-art method (W2A16) by 5.8 in terms of perplexity on C4 and by 8.2% in terms of average accuracy on zero-shot tasks.
  </details>

- **[NovaSplat: Model-Free 6D Object Pose Estimation via 3D Gaussian Splatting Onboarding and Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13309)**  `arXiv:2610.13309`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Georgios Floros, Evangelos Sartinas, Orestis Vangelis, Maria Pateraki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  6D pose estimation of novel objects without CAD models remains an open challenge: model-free methods must build the object representation from scratch during onboarding and have long trailed their model-based counterparts. We present NovaSplat, a model-free pipeline that closes this gap. From a short smartphone video captured at onboarding, we train a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) model that serves both as a template source for detection and coarse pose estimation and as a differentiable backbone for pose refinement. Our refiner optimizes the object-to-camera pose with combined photometric and geometric losses under a continuous rotation parametrization free of gradient singularities. On a household-object benchmark, NovaSplat surpasses all model-based methods. On a challenging industrial benchmark with textureless and specular surfaces, it outperforms all model-free competitors while substantially narrowing the gap to model-based ones. We further introduce NovaSplat-Lite, an inference variant that is $3.1\times$ faster at negligible accuracy cost. Code will be made available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Golden Anchor for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13217)**  `arXiv:2610.13217`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinyuan Xu, Shitong Shao, Lichen Bai, Zikai Zhou, Guoxi Huang, Nantheera Anantrasirichai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation supports digital content creation, with applications in virtual avatars, AI-driven filmmaking, interactive game engines, and world models. State-of-the-art systems achieve controllability primarily through image-to-video (I2V) generation, in which an injected anchor image determines the identity, composition, and style of the resulting video. In modern pipelines, this anchor is synthesized by a text-to-image (T2I) generator. This raises a largely overlooked question: Are images obtained directly from the T2I model truly suitable for current video models? Our analysis shows that anchor aesthetic and quality scores are weakly correlated with downstream video quality. The structure-and-texture score is negatively correlated with I2V video quality in both the visual and motion dimensions. Fine-tuning the T2I model on an image reward accordingly yields anchors with visibly denser structural and character detail: they score higher on image metrics, yet lower the video reward on every I2V model we test and change downstream motion unpredictably. We therefore introduce Golden Anchor Learning (GAL), to our knowledge the first framework for learning golden anchors by optimizing a T2I generator with downstream video feedback. GAL evaluates candidate anchors through the videos they induce under a frozen I2V model and converts video rewards into image-level preferences for DPO fine-tuning of the T2I generator. On all four I2V models, GAL raises the VBench average by $2.97\%$--$8.46\%$ over the untuned generator. The rendered clips also score higher under VideoReward-Overall, and their extracted frames under HPSv3 and UniPercept. GAL yields consistent motion gains across all four I2V models, as measured by a $5.62\%$--$38.24\%$ increase in VBench dynamic degree. Although GAL is trained only on HunyuanVideo-1.5 renders, these gains extend to the other three I2V models.
  </details>

- **[H-OPD: Confidence Aware Heterogeneous Multi-Teacher Multimodal On-policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02592)**  `arXiv:2607.02592`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Qixiang Yin, Huanjin Yao, Yuchen Cai, Jianghao Chen, Ziyi Wang, Fei Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has recently emerged as an effective post-training paradigm by providing supervision on student-generated trajectories. However, existing OPD methods for multimodal reasoning usually rely on a static teacher routing, assigning each sample to a single teacher based on modality or task type. This ignores that visual grounding and abstract reasoning may dominate different decoding steps, making a single teacher insufficient for the full trajectory. To this end, H-OPD is proposed as a confidence-aware heterogeneous multi-teacher OPD framework for multimodal reasoning. By verifying the complementarity of heterogeneous teachers in the same reasoning process, H-OPD replaces task or sample level teacher routing with token-level teacher arbitration along the shared student trajectory. H-OPD employs vision-to-language description transfer to enable text-only teachers to access key visual semantics, and uses a confidence-aware arbitration mechanism to dynamically combine vision-language teacher and text-only teachers at each token. Extensive evaluations over 11 widely-used reasoning benchmarks showcase the superior performance of our method.
  </details>

- **[Vision2Code: A Multi-Domain Benchmark for Evaluating Image-to-Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11307)**  `arXiv:2605.11307`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ajay Vikram Periasami, Junlin Wang, Bhuwan Dhingra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study whether vision-language models (VLMs) can reconstruct structured, graphics-based images by writing executable code to generate them. Existing benchmarks on image-to-code generation are generally limited in the domains they cover, rely on reference source code for evaluation, and do not provide metrics to quantify domain-specific issues.
